| **148** | **A SKILL'S PLAN DOES NOT STOP WHEN A STEP FAILS, AND THE STEP AFTER IT ANSWERS FROM WHATEVER WAS LEFT SELECTED IN REVIT.** Found 2026-09-19, running the `count-elements` plan end to end on `test projject` (session 36216) - the first time any skill's composition has been run against a model, using the job file [row 141](#)'s session generated. **MEASURED, BOTH WAYS.** Handed values the FIRST step could not bind, `filter-elements-by-category` refused with `needs_request_values` - and `count-elements` **RAN ANYWAY AND RETURNED `count 1`, `countedNothing false`**, a confident number a modeller would believe. The 1 was a floor left selected by an unrelated probe minutes earlier. `prove` printed *"1 ran, 1 failed"* underneath it. **THIS IS THE SKILL'S OWN STATED HAZARD ARRIVING BY A ROUTE IT DID NOT DESCRIBE** - `count-elements` says *"a count that silently filtered to nothing looks exactly like a model with nothing in it"*, and this is worse: the filter did not run at all and the count answered from a stale selection. **THE BINDING NOTE IS HONEST AND IS THE ONLY THING THAT SAYS SO** - it read `<- elements from the selection (1)` rather than `from filter-elements-by-category`, which is exactly the line `fragment-proving` rule 5 says to judge a run on. So the machinery does not lie; it simply does not stop, and a reader who trusts the number without reading the note has no way to tell. **THE SAME PLAN WITH VALUES THAT BIND WORKS PERFECTLY**, which is the other half and the reason this is a finding about composition rather than about either fragment: `category=Ducts, levelId=Level 1` gave `filter-elements-by-category` **8 matching**, `count-elements` reported **`count 8`** and its note read **`<- elements from filter-elements-by-category (8)`**, and `prove` printed *"2 ran, 0 failed"*. The hand-over is real | **OPEN, and it belongs to whoever owns [row 141](#)** - the skill runner that does not exist yet. Recorded here rather than fixed because `prove` is a diagnostic that judges nothing, and the decision - does a failed step STOP the plan, or does the next step refuse a chain it did not receive - is a design question about skills, not a fragment defect. **NOTHING ABOUT EITHER FRAGMENT IS WRONG.** Both are PROVEN, both behaved exactly as declared, and `count-elements` counting the current selection when nothing was handed to it is its documented behaviour. **THE CHEAPEST REPAIR IS PROBABLY THE CHAIN EXPECTATION THAT ALREADY EXISTS** - `expect_from` ([docs/36](36-remembering-between-steps.md)) makes a consumer NAME the producer it expects and refuses when something else left the values, which is this exact failure one layer down and already built and proved. A skill runner that emitted `expect_from` between its steps would get this for free. **AND A CORRECTION TO THIS ROW, MADE BEFORE ANYBODY ACTED ON IT.** It first said the generated job files carry the wrong values for their first step - that `count-elements`'s `set:` held `categoryName` and `inViewOnly` where `filter-elements-by-category` needs `category` and `levelId`. **THAT WAS WRONG AND THE GENERATOR IS RIGHT.** The file sets `category` and `levelId` PER JOB, exactly as that step declares; `categoryName` and `inViewOnly` sit in `defaults.set` because they belong to the SETUP CHAIN, `select-by-category-name` then `set-selection`, which is also correct. **The failing first step above was this session running `prove` BY HAND with the setup chain's values instead of the step's own** - my mistake, not the generator's, and the finding about the plan not stopping stands on its own either way. Written down because a peer sent chasing a defect that is not there costs more than the finding is worth |
| **149** | **WAS 142, THEN 143, THEN 147, AND IS 149 - THE REGISTER'S OWN HAZARD, FOUR TIMES IN ONE DAY.** The skills session took 142 (#199) and then 143 to 146 (#201), each reaching `main` first, and each time this row moved. **THE RULE THAT WOULD HAVE STOPPED IT IS THE ONE ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN** - read the last row on `main` and ANNOUNCE the number you are taking - and the half nobody follows is the ANNOUNCE. 140 and 141 were handed over in writing and were the only two that did not collide. The rule that would have avoided it is the one already written down: read the last row on `main` and ANNOUNCE the number you are taking. 140 and 141 were handed over in writing and 142 was not. **A SETUP CHAIN AND THE FRAGMENT UNDER TEST CANNOT BE GIVEN DIFFERENT VALUES FOR A NAME THEY SHARE, AND THE COLLISION IS SILENT.** Found 2026-09-19 arranging `select-touching`. It needs `target` (one element) and `categories` (what to look for); the obvious chain is `select-in-region`, whose own selector need is ALSO called `categories`. **There is nowhere to say they differ.** `tools/batch-prove.py:227` merges `defaults.set` with the job's `set` into ONE flat dict and `:441` emits one `--set` per key; `mcp/client/heron_bridge_client.py:1515` then does `chosen_setup = values` and hands **the same dict to every setup step AND to the fragment**. So `categories: Floors` for the chain and `categories: Walls` for the question collapse to one, and the dry run's argv said so plainly: **a single `--set categories=Walls`**. **CAUGHT BY READING THE ARGV, NOT BY A FAILURE.** In this case it would have failed loudly afterwards - the region would have selected 28 walls and `target` binds exactly one - **but that is luck, not a guard.** Where the fragment's need is a LIST, the chain silently selects the wrong population, the job RUNS, and it reports a number about something nobody asked for. That is the confident-wrong result this register exists for. **MEASURED ACROSS THE WHOLE LIBRARY RATHER THAN LEFT AS ONE STORY: 88 fragment/chain pairs share at least one caller-supplied need name, 16 of them on a DRAFT fragment.** `categories` is the common one (`select-in-region`, `select-by-categories`, `select-by-parameter-value` all take it), then `parameterName`, then `inViewOnly` and `levelId`. **IT HAS NOT BITTEN BEFORE BECAUSE OF HOW THE PROOFS WERE ARRANGED, not because it is rare**: a fragment needing `categories` was proved with `select-by-category-name`, which takes `categoryName` and does not collide. Nobody chose that on purpose | **FIXED 2026-09-19 - AND THE FIRST VERSION OF THE FIX HAD A P1 IN IT, FOUND BY REVIEW ON PR #198.** **`--negative-setup-set` COULD NOT WORK AT ALL**, which is the sharpest kind of defect this file can carry: a proof that RAN, PASSED, and was about something else. `arrange()` inferred the phase from `using`, and a negative differing ONLY in its arrangement leaves `negative_values` empty - so `using` arrived `None`, `arrange` read that as the positive phase, and the negative leg was recorded against the POSITIVE setup. The phase is passed in explicitly now and never inferred. `cmd_validate` also gated the whole negative leg on `negative_in or negative_values`, so such a proof fell through to the interactive prompt and waited at a keyboard nobody was at. **AND `batch-prove` HELD THE SAME GAP ONE LAYER UP, WHICH ONLY RUNNING IT FOUND** - its own check demanded `negative-set:` or `negative-in:` and refused the job before the client was ever called, naming the two keys it knew. The client's half had already been repaired and the batch runner still said no. Reading would not have caught that one. **PROVED ON A MODEL, BOTH LEGS, AND THE RECORD NAMES THEM APART**: `test projject`, session 36216, the two legs differing ONLY in `negative-setup-set` - positive bound `target` to **'600 x 600mm Grid' (id 926468)**, the one ceiling; negative bound it to **'Generic 300mm' (id 926428)**, the one floor; and the negative's `arranged` line reads *"run arranged with categories=Floors..."*. Before the repair the negative would have silently re-used the ceiling. **ONE CASE IS REFUSED RATHER THAN FIXED, AND IT IS WRITTEN DOWN AS SUCH.** A setup step at MODIFY or above travels WITH the fragment in the request's `setup` array, which carries a name, a source and needs - no values - and `RunSetupSteps` binds every step from the run's top-level `supplied`. So `setup-set` could be accepted, sent, and quietly not applied, leaving the original silent collision alive for WRITE arrangements. Carrying per-step values needs the ADD-IN to read them, which is C# and costs a rebuild and a Revit restart. Until then the combination is REFUSED by name, so it is a wrong answer that cannot be produced rather than one produced silently. **AND THE STORED DIGEST NOW CARRIES AN `INDEX_FORMAT` VERSION** - without it an upgrade that changes how `heron_search.index()` derives anything would still match the digest the PREVIOUS version wrote, and since `_Open` never forces, the old tables would be served until some unrelated fragment happened to change. **THE ORIGINAL ACCOUNT FOLLOWS.** **FIXED 2026-09-19, AND THE FIX IS ADDITIVE SO NOT ONE EXISTING PROOF CHANGES.** `setup-set:` and `negative-setup-set:` in a job file become `--setup-set` / `--negative-setup-set` on `validate`, and `arrange()` uses them for the setup steps ONLY when they are present - **absent, every step gets exactly what it got before**, which is why no job file written before today needs touching. Two files: `tools/batch-prove.py` (job keys plus argv) and `mcp/client/heron_bridge_client.py` (parser, signature, `arrange`). **THE PARSER NOW ROUTES BY A LOOKUP TABLE RATHER THAN BY SPELLING, AND THAT WAS FORCED BY THIS CHANGE**: the old rules were `token.endswith("set")` and `token.startswith("--negative")`, and `--negative-setup-set` satisfies BOTH, so two string rules that had agreed for a year stopped agreeing. There is a check for exactly that. **PROVED ON A MODEL, NOT JUST IN A TEST** - `test projject`, session 27244: the chain was given `categories: Ceilings` while the fragment was asked about `categories: Ducts`, and the run bound `target` to **'600 x 600mm Grid' (id 926468)** - the one ceiling - while the question stayed on ducts. Before this, that job could not be written down at all. Five checks in `tests/test_caller_values.py`, including that absent leaves both lists empty. **THE ORIGINAL STATE LINE FOLLOWS, because the workaround it describes is still what any job file written before today is doing.** **OPEN, and the workaround used today is written down because it is not a fix.** `select-touching` was re-arranged onto `select-by-category-name` purely because `categoryName` does not collide with `categories` - and that chain is VIEW-SCOPED, which promptly cost a second run: `categoryName: Ceilings` in a FloorPlan returned nothing, because ceilings do not draw in a floor plan, and the refusal read *"NOTHING is selected"*. **Trading a silent collision for a view-scoping trap is not a repair.** **THE FIX IS ADDITIVE AND CARRIES NO RISK TO ANY EXISTING PROOF** - built the same day, see the head of this cell. **WHAT WAS ESTABLISHED ABOUT THE FRAGMENT ITSELF, since it ran twice**: `select-touching` means OVERLAP and says so in its own words - *"Nothing OVERLAPS it. That is not the same as nothing being related"* - so a floor and the walls it sits against correctly return 0. It bound its target properly (`'Generic 300mm' (id 926428)`), which is the half that was in doubt. It needs two things that genuinely interpenetrate, and the ducts passing through walls in this model are exactly that - it is the SINGLE target that is hard to arrange, not the question. **ONE MORE THING WORTH KNOWING**: `batch-prove` deletes the run record before each run ([row 87](#)'s fix, so a missing file means nothing ran), so a probe re-run DESTROYS the previous record. Copy it first if the evidence matters |

| **151** | **D-53 TRACKING HAS BEEN JUDGED FOR A FRAGMENT SINCE THE DECISION WAS WRITTEN, AND NOTHING HAS EVER PRODUCED ONE.** Measured 2026-09-19. `brain/heron_validate.py` reads `record["tracking"]`, refuses fewer than three rows in its own words - *"the tracking set has only %d row(s). D-53 asks for the answer to follow the input across SEVERAL different inputs; two cannot show that"* - and refuses rows that all came back the same, *"which is what tracking exists to rule out"*. **`grep -n tracking mcp/client/heron_bridge_client.py` returns NOTHING.** No command writes those rows, so a `record["tracking"]` could only ever be typed by hand. **The judging half was built and the producing half was not**, and the gap stayed quiet because the fragments that need it are exactly the fragments nobody could prove. `tools/prove-agent.py vary` is the working example one layer up and has done this for an ADD-IN AGENT since 2026-09-19; the fragment side had no equivalent. **[docs/NEEDS-CHECKING.md Group W](NEEDS-CHECKING.md) found the same gap from the other end**, reading the skills' own job files, and called it *"THE HIGHEST-LEVERAGE THING LEFT IN THE PROVING MACHINERY"*: **three fragments - `trace-connectivity`, `report-findings` and `describe-blank-parameters` - stand between three skills and three more proofs, and all three are shapes `batch-prove`'s rule cannot judge** | **OPEN - HALF BUILT 2026-09-19, and the state cell says OPEN on purpose.** `tools/open-defects.py` counts a row by whether its state STARTS with the word, and the runs are genuinely still owed - a cell reading only *HALF BUILT* would drop this out of the sweep while the unfinished half sat there, which is the shape [row 118](#) is about. **Which half is built is the whole point.** `tools/prove-tracking.py` does every check that can be made **before a model is opened**, and `--dry-run` answers completely: a fragment already at PROVEN, a need that is not `source: request`, a shape D-54 cannot receive, fewer than three **DISTINCT** values, another caller value not held still, and a `--set` the fragment does not declare (which is [row 71](#)'s rule applied one step earlier). **THE JUDGEMENT IS IMPORTED, NOT RE-DECIDED** - `contract_of`, `looks_empty` and `_as_count` come from `heron_validate`, the module that reads the record back, because two copies of *what counts as an answer* is how a runner and a drafter start disagreeing about the same fragment on the same day. **WHAT A FRAGMENT HAS THAT AN AGENT HAS NOT DECIDES THE DESIGN**: `prove-agent.py vary` collects every number in the reply because an agent has no contract saying which is the answer; a fragment's `provides` does, so this tracks the DECLARED result and refuses to guess when more than one is declared. **ALL THREE OF GROUP W's FRAGMENTS ARE NOW ARRANGEABLE, PROVED IN `tests/test_prove_tracking.py`** - `trace-connectivity` varying `tolerance` with `start=selected` held still, `report-findings` varying `whatWasChecked` with `checkedCount` held, `describe-blank-parameters` varying `parameterName`. **AND GROUP W IS TOO PESSIMISTIC ABOUT ONE OF THEM**: it reads `describe-blank-parameters` as having *"nothing for the rule to look at"* because it declares no explicit `role:`, and `heron_fragment.provide_role` treats an absent `role:` as **`result`** - the stricter read - so `findings` is trackable. **THE RUNS ARE BUILT TOO, AND EXACTLY ONE FUNCTION IS UNEXERCISED.** `live_runner` resolves the source and contract, opens one bridge and sends **`cmd_prove`'s own call** - same operation, same three arguments, same `chain: "reset"` on the first and not after, same timeout - because that call has run against real models for weeks and a second opinion about how to talk to the executor is the last thing this file should contribute. With no Revit it refuses cleanly: *"NOT RUN - No Revit is connected. Press Heron on the ribbon to connect first."* **A run that cannot pass `judge()` WRITES NOTHING** - a record that looks like a proof and is not is worse than having none - and one that passes is written where `heron_validate` reads it, which then asks the same two questions again before a person signs. **`live_runner` is the one function no test here can reach**, and the tool's own docstring names it rather than leaving a reader to find out. **It never signs and never promotes**, and tracking is not an easier route past D-30: it is the substitute only where no arrangement makes the answer empty, which is D-53's whole condition **THE RUN LOOP IS BUILT TOO, AND IT IS PROVED ON A FAKE RUNNER.** `run_rows()` takes the runner as an ARGUMENT, so the loop, the refusal handling and the row building are all exercised on a machine with no Revit - `tests/test_prove_tracking.py` passes a fake that returns known replies. **The only line left unproved is the bridge call itself**, which is the same call `cmd_prove` already makes. It also proves the rule `tools/prove-agent.py vary` states in one sentence and this inherits: **a refused value is not a tracking row** - it shows the fragment never looked, not that it looked and found nothing - so refusals are reported, do not count toward the three, and a set left short by them says so rather than passing. **AND A WRITE IS NOT TRACKED DOWN THE READ PATH.** `write_path_refusal()` refuses anything at or above the write threshold, and the threshold is **read from `HeronOperationRegistry.cs`** through `generate-jobs.write_threshold()` rather than typed - Golden Rule 19, where risk is looked up by name and never supplied by a caller. Two reasons, both already in this register: a write varied three ways changes the model three times, and D-53 has no equivalent of the `TransactionGroup` `validate` holds; and sending a write down the read path is [defect row 6](#), which cost half a morning by arriving silently. A risk that is not a level HeronRisk names is refused rather than assumed safe. Verified: `set-view-scale` (MODIFY) is refused with that reason, `report-findings` (SUGGEST) is sent **DEFERRED BY THE OWNER 2026-09-22**, at the close of the session that found it: not now, later, and he said later is better. It stays OPEN because the defect is still in the file - **open here means the defect exists, not that somebody is part-way through it.** Nothing is blocked and nothing was left half-changed; the tool is exactly as it was found. Pick it up when he asks. |

| **155** | **A TEST SUITE BREAKS 55 REAL `fragment.yaml` FILES FOR TWELVE SECONDS OF EVERY `check-gaps` RUN, AND A `finally` IS NOT A PROMISE A KILLED PROCESS KEEPS.** Reported by the session validating the Windows install (PR #200), which watched `git status` go from 6 modified files to 61 and back while `tools/check-gaps.py` was running. **REPRODUCED EXACTLY**, 2026-09-19: a poller sampling `git status --porcelain -- brain/fragments` caught the window and listed the same 55 cards, `select-with-warnings`, `select-without-level` and `ungroup-elements` among them. **THE WRITER WAS FOUND BY A SWEEP, NOT BY A GREP**, and that is the part worth keeping: a static search for anything writing `brain/fragments` finds nothing, because the write is one `io.open(path, "w")` inside a closure over paths that are derived rather than spelled. So every suite under `tests/` was re-run under a `sys.addaudithook` logging every write beneath `brain/fragments/` - no total typed here, because this register has been wrong about that one twice. **EXACTLY ONE SUITE WRITES THE REAL LIBRARY: `tests/test_graph.py`, in `break_providers()`.** It has to show the deriver catching a broken contract, so it renamed `- name: elements` in the `provides:` block of every one of the 55 fragments that provide it, asked the graph, and wrote the originals back. **Measured: 67s for the suite, the real library wrong for 11.9s of it - 18% of the run** - sitting inside a sweep that takes about eight minutes. **TWO THINGS WERE WRONG AND ONLY ONE OF THEM WAS ABOUT EXCEPTIONS.** The `finally` was already there and already correct, so every path that RETURNS puts the files back. But `check-gaps.py` runs each suite through `subprocess.run` with `SUITE_TIMEOUT = 300`, and a timeout **kills** the child; so does Ctrl+Break, and so does the machine. `finally` runs through none of them, and what survives is 55 fragments whose contract says `somethingElse`, with nothing on disk saying why. **AND NOTHING HAS TO CRASH AT ALL**: several sessions share this checkout and its worktrees, and one that runs `git add -A` inside those 11.9 seconds commits 55 `fragment.yaml` changes nobody made and is told nothing. That is [ROW 131](#) one layer out - a shared thing left as whichever process got there last wrote it - except that here the shared thing is the repository itself. **THE HOUSE PATTERN WAS ALREADY IN THE SAME FOLDER**: the same sweep shows `test_docs_guard` and `test_metadata_guard` each copying the whole repository into the system temp folder and breaking THAT, 1,185 files apiece. One file did not follow it. **THE FINGERPRINT WAS NOT INVOLVED**, which was the first guess and is worth recording as excluded: `heron_fragment.fingerprint()` only reads, `proof_is_stale()` only compares, and the one thing that rewrites a `fingerprint:` line in a real card is `heron_validate.restamp(apply_changes=True)`, reachable only from `python brain/heron_validate.py restamp --apply` - no test and no checker calls it, and the sweep logged not one write from it. **ONE OTHER SUITE TOUCHES A REAL CARD AND IS LEFT ALONE**: `test_embed.py` calls `os.utime` on `set-selection/fragment.yaml` to prove indexing is content-hashed rather than timed. mtime only, no content, git sees nothing, and it is the whole point of that test. | **Fixed 2026-09-20 - `tests/test_graph.py` and `brain/heron_graph.py`.** The break now happens in a throwaway copy: `stand_in_library()` writes all 395 `fragment.yaml` into a root under the system temp folder in 2.0s, and nothing in the suite opens a file under `brain/` for writing. It now takes `fragments=`, the way `composes_into()` and `composes_from()` already did, and both existing callers are untouched. **Copying only `fragment.yaml` and not the `impl/` trees is a bet on what `load()` reads, so the suite checks it rather than assuming**: the copy must hold the same 395 fragments with the same 0 problems, and `brain/fragments` must answer `composes_into` identically, before anything is broken. **Re-measured: 58,336 reads of a watched card across a 63s run, never once broken**, against 11.9s before. **Worth settling, and NOT fixed:** nothing in this repository would have caught it. No gate runs the suites and then asks whether the tree changed, which is the check that turns this whole class - a test that edits the repository it is testing - from something a person happens to notice into something that fails **Written as 142, which `trace-system` had already taken in #199, and renumbered on merge - [ROW 149](#) counted four of these collisions in one day and this is the fifth. Its first test keeps a row where `rung(risk[winner]) > rung(risk[claimer])`; a fragment declaring its OWN sentence and winning has winner == claimer, so the comparison is false and there is no crossing by construction. Its second opens `if risk_of(store, fid) != "READ": continue`, so a sentence claimed by a **MODIFY** is skipped on the first line - and two lines later it says `if served == fid: continue` with the belief written beside it: **`# the host answers correctly`**. That comment is the whole defect in four words. It is right about every case the tool was built for and wrong about this one, and nothing in the file is at fault - the question was never asked of it. `check-risk-crossings.py` asks sentences **nobody declares**, precisely because the first can only test what is declared - so a declared one is not in its list. **Checked rather than assumed: of its 78 questions, ZERO are among these ten.** And the near-miss is the whole story. Its closest sentence to *"what scale is this view"* is **"what is the scale of this view"** - five words shared, one phrasing apart - and [row 146](#) reports that one crossing to `SET_VIEW_SCALE` and calls it a **capability gap**, which it also is. **So two sweeps found two halves of one defect from opposite sides, a week apart, and neither could see the other half.** The hand-written phrasing crosses because nothing READ answers it; the declared phrasing crosses because the WRITE claims it in its own `utterances:` block. The first needs a new capability. The second needs one line deleted from a card. A reader given only row 146 would have built the fragment and left the declaration in place, and the sentence Ajmal actually says would have gone on landing on a write by identity, which no new READ can outrank. **It is [row 137](#)'s blind spot one layer down**, and like that one it needs no search at all to find: it is a property of the card. **ALL TEN, SWEPT OVER THE LIBRARY**: `SET_VIEW_SCALE` *"what scale is this view"*; `SET_VIEW_RANGE` *"what is the view range"*; `SET_PRINT_SETTINGS` *"what paper sizes can i print"*; `SET_DATUM_EXTENT_TYPE` *"is this grid 2d or 3d here"*; `ASSIGN_LOCATION_DATA` *"which room is each device in"*; `EXTRACT_CAD_CURVES` *"what layers are in this cad file"* and *"...in this dwg"*; `DIMENSION_FAMILY_INSTANCES` *"how far apart are these on the drawing"*; `DIMENSION_MEP_RUNS` *"how far apart are these pipes on the drawing"*; and `REPLACE_MATERIAL` *"why will this material not purge"*. **TWO ARE WORTH A SECOND LOOK RATHER THAN A REPAIR**, which is why this reports and does not judge: the DIMENSION pair say *on the drawing*, which is arguably an instruction to annotate; and `REPLACE_MATERIAL`'s is a CONSIDERED DECISION with its reasoning in a comment above it - it was moved there in 2026-09-06 because it had been resolving to `FIND_UNUSED_MATERIALS`, *"which lists materials with NO user, and therefore cannot say what is still holding on to THIS one"*. Found 2026-09-19.** The state says OPEN on purpose and `tools/open-defects.py` counts a row by its first word: the ten sentences are still declared on writes today, and a cell opening *the sweep is built* would drop this out of the sweep while the unrepaired half sat there - which is [row 118](#)'s shape and [row 153](#)'s. **WHAT IS BUILT:** `tools/check-declared-questions.py` reads the FILES through `heron_fragment.load_all()` - **never the store**, so it holds in CI where there is none and two runs disagree only if somebody edited a fragment ([rows 116](#) and [136](#)). The write line is read from `HeronOperationRegistry.cs` through `generate-jobs.write_threshold()` - Golden Rule 19, imported and not copied, because that function REFUSES to guess when the registry is rearranged - and a risk `HeronRisk` does not name is **reported rather than assumed safe** (D-52). It is now in `gates.yml`'s *reports* job beside the other three, so it runs on every pull request instead of when somebody remembers. **AN IMPERATIVE IS NOT A QUESTION, AND THE PATTERN WAS CORRECTED BEFORE IT LANDED**: the first version included `do` and `did` as auxiliaries and filed *"do the grayout"* as a defect on the fragment that correctly owns it. Both cases are pinned in `tests/test_declared_questions.py`, and a sentence that asks and **then says what to do** - *"which elbow this type inserts, change it"* - is listed separately and never counted, which is the separation `check-risk-crossings.py` already makes for imperatives. **EVERY SENTENCE IN THAT SUITE IS A REAL DECLARED UTTERANCE**, not an invented one: an invented sentence proves only that the pattern matches itself. **IT NEVER SUGGESTS DELETING AN UTTERANCE TO TIDY THE REPORT** - [row 113](#)'s forbidden move is weakening a declaration to buy a number, and deleting a sentence a modeller really says so a sweep comes back clean is the mirror of it. **NOTHING IN `brain/fragments/` WAS TOUCHED**: the ten are a finding for whoever owns those files, and the repair for each is one line - declare the sentence on the READ that should own it - **except where no READ exists**, which is [row 146](#)'s capability gap and a larger thing. **AND ROW 146's ENUMERATION IS RIGHT WHILE THE CONCLUSION DRAWN FROM IT IS TOO STRONG** - measured the same way, and the distinction matters because the repair is different. This sweep asks nothing about reach. **SOME OF THE FIVE ARE PROBABLY RIGHT AND THE TOOL SAYS SO IN ITS OWN OUTPUT**: *"how many sprinklers do I need"* may genuinely belong to a layout skill, because answering it IS the layout - unlike *"how many sprinklers on level 2"*, which is a count. That is a judgement for the owner, and **this session owns `brain/skills/*.yaml` and edited none of them**: deleting a sentence Ajmal really says to make a list shorter is [row 113](#)'s forbidden move held up to a mirror **THE RULE EXISTS NOW: [Q-57](OPEN-QUESTIONS.md) IS ANSWERED, 2026-09-21, AS [D-86](DECISIONS.md).** A write may declare a question **only when the question cannot be answered without performing the write**. He chose it over a blanket ban and over leaving it unruled. **THIS ROW'S OWN JUDGEMENT IS NOW THE TEST, NOT A GUESS**: *"how many sprinklers do I need"* stays on the layout skill, because answering it IS the layout; *"what scale is this view"* leaves `SET_VIEW_SCALE`, because a scale can be read without touching it. **AND THE ORDER MATTERS, WHICH IS WHY NOTHING IS EDITED HERE YET:** a sentence that leaves a write needs somewhere to go first, and the starkest case is the worst one - [row 146](#) measured a view's scale as the library's ONE CERTAIN capability gap, so removing that sentence before something can answer it drops it into ranking, where it may find a different writer. **Build the READ, then move the sentence.** The fifteen still need judging one at a time; what has changed is that there is now a test to judge them against, and `tools/check-declared-questions.py` is a report a reader can close |

## 5b. HERON'S OWN DEFECTS found by reading the repository, file by file

> Section 5 holds what **proving against a model** found. This holds what **reading** found — the word-by-word sweep of every tracked file that is not `brain/**.yaml`, recorded in [`REVIEW-LEDGER.tsv`](REVIEW-LEDGER.tsv). **How many that is, and how many have been read, is derived and not typed here.**
>
> **They are kept apart because they are found differently and they go stale differently.** A proving defect is re-tested by running the fragment again. A reading defect is re-tested by reading the file again — and the ledger knows when that is necessary, because the file's content hash stopped matching the one recorded when it was read.
>
> **No number is typed here.** Derive it:
>
> ```bash
> python tools/open-defects.py          # both sections, by id
> python tools/review-ledger.py         # how much of the repository has been read
> ```

**THE SWEEP RECORDS, IT DOES NOT REPAIR.** A file found wrong is marked `issue` in the ledger and written up here; the file itself is left alone. A sweep that also fixes things stops being a sweep — the reader loses track of which files have actually been read, and a repair made in passing is a change nobody reviewed. Anything big enough to need its own sitting gets **`OPEN — needs a sitting`** and waits there.

**A ROW HERE IS WRITTEN FOR A SESSION THAT WAS NOT PRESENT.** Name the file and the line; say what is wrong in the words you would use out loud; and say what you did **not** check, so the next reader knows where your reading stopped rather than assuming it was complete.

| # | Defect | State |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | `HeronPaths.IsSafeToDelete` COMPARES PATHS AS TEXT, SO A FOLDER MERELY NAMED LIKE HERON'S READS AS… — [full row](fragment-issues-archive/reading-defects-5b-001-025.md#row-5b-1) | **FIXED 2026-09-21, one separator and a suite that RUNS it.** |
| **2** | THE STOP REFUSAL TELLS THE USER TO PRESS A BUTTON THAT WAS REMOVED, AND SENDS THEM AWAY FROM THE… — [full row](fragment-issues-archive/reading-defects-5b-001-025.md#row-5b-2) | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - AND THIS ROW'S OWN PROPOSED REPAIR WAS WRONG, WHICH IS THE USEFUL PART.** |
| **3** | **`NaN` REACHES A PARAMETER WRITE AND A GEOMETRY POINT, BECAUSE TWO SITES HAND-ROLLED THE RANGE CHECK INSTEAD OF CALLING THE GUARD THAT EXISTS.** [`revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs:1928`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs) (the point parser) and [`:2578`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitFragment.cs) (a `length` parameter value) both parse with `double.TryParse(…, NumberStyles.Float, InvariantCulture, …)` and then test `millimetres > HeronUnits.MaxMillimetres \|\| millimetres < -HeronUnits.MaxMillimetres`. **Both comparisons are FALSE for `NaN`**, so the check passes it, and the next line is `HeronUnits.MillimetresToFeet(millimetres)` — into `new DoubleParameterValue(…)` at 2588, and into `new XYZ(…)` at 1941. **MEASURED, NOT REASONED — AND THE FIRST MEASUREMENT NAMED NO RUNTIME, WHICH WAS A MISTAKE CORRECTED WHEN THIS WAS FIXED.** A console program compiled against **net8.0** ran the exact parse and the exact comparison: `"NaN"`, `"nan"` and `"NAN"` **all parse true** and **all pass the inline guard**; `"Infinity"` and `"-Infinity"` are correctly rejected by it, and `"200000000"` is rejected as intended. `MillimetresToFeet(NaN)` returns `NaN`. **Re-measured on .NET Framework 4.8 — which is what Revit 2020 to 2024 actually run** (`Directory.Build.props`: net472 for 2020, net48 for 2021-2024, net8.0-windows for 2025-2026, net10.0-windows for 2027) — **`"nan"` and `"NAN"` parse FALSE there**, because only .NET Core made the culture's `NaNSymbol` match case-insensitively. **The exact spelling `"NaN"` parses on BOTH**, so the defect was real on every release Heron supports; the lowercase spellings were only ever a way in on **2025 and later**. D-05 does not extrapolate a runtime table, and the original sentence here did. So the hand-rolled guard catches every case it was written for **except** the one that has no ordering. **THE GUARD THAT CATCHES IT IS TEN LINES AWAY AND IS NOT CALLED.** [`HeronUnits.IsUsableMillimetres`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronUnits.cs) rejects all three spellings — the same program confirms it — and its own docstring states this exact hazard: *"A NaN reaching ElementTransformUtils is a geometry operation with no defined result, inside a transaction, on the user's model."* **Of six `MillimetresToFeet` call sites in the repository, exactly one calls the guard** ([`RevitWrite.cs:742`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs)); one converts a literal `1.0`; and the remaining four rely on upstream checks, two of which are these. **REACHABILITY IS THE PART THAT MATTERS.** These parse text supplied as a fragment argument — a parameter value or a point typed or composed upstream. `length NaN` is not an exotic input for a value that arrived from a division; nothing between the text and the transaction re-checks it. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether Revit itself refuses a `NaN` `DoubleParameterValue` or an `XYZ` with a `NaN` ordinate — that needs a real model and is the one question this row cannot answer from source, so **the damage is unestablished while the path to it is proven**. `RevitFragment.cs` has NOT been read in full (4,256 lines); this was reached by tracing every `MillimetresToFeet` call, so **there may be more of these** and the file is still marked unread in the ledger. **THE REPAIR IS TO DELETE BOTH HAND-ROLLED CHECKS AND CALL `IsUsableMillimetres`**, keeping each site's own wording for the refusal — the message is good, the test underneath it is not | **FIXED 2026-09-20, on the owner's instruction, and the fix is to ASK THE PART THAT OWNS THE ANSWER.** Both sites now refuse `NaN` and both infinities with their own sentence — a modeller is told *"is not a real number… a value like that usually arrives from a calculation that did not work out"*, not a bound it did not breach — and the range test underneath is `!HeronUnits.IsUsableMillimetres(...)` rather than a second hand-written pair of comparisons. That is the point of the repair: the hole existed because the bound was written **twice**, once in the part that owns it and once beside the call, and only one copy knew about `NaN`. **Compiled on all eight releases, 2020 through 2027, all seven projects, `tools/check-compile.py` exit 0.** **NOT YET RUN IN REVIT** — compiling is the API surface agreeing and nothing more, and it costs a Revit restart to try. What would settle it is one `length NaN` against a real model, refused with the new sentence |
| **4** | **SMALL, AND WORDING ONLY: the write refusal points at "the lock button", and the ribbon button is labelled "Changes".** [`platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs:126`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs) — *"To turn it on, use the **lock button** on the Heron AI ribbon"*. The button is created at [`HeronApplication.cs:286`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs) with the text **`"Changes"`**; nothing on the ribbon is called *lock*. **THE PADLOCK LARGELY RESCUES IT** — the button's own long description is *"Closed padlock: Heron can read the model and nothing else. Open padlock: Heron may change it"*, so a modeller reading *"the lock button"* and seeing a padlock will click the right thing. **This is recorded because the register's standard is that a refusal names its control exactly** (the same standard row 2 fails harder), not because anyone is likely to be lost by it. Unlike row 2 this message is **live** — it is what a user sees whenever a write is refused. **The repair is one word**, and which word is the owner's | **PARTLY FIXED 2026-09-21 — the RIBBON PATH was wrong too, and that half is not a judgement.** [#213](https://github.com/Ajmalpshaik/Heron-AI/pull/213) renamed the tab to **`Heron`** and the panel to **`AI Bridge`**, and corrected the messages on the Python side — `heron_session.py`, `heron_mcp_server.py` and the bridge client all now say *"Heron > AI Bridge > Heron"*. **It left the two in `platform/Heron.Core` behind**, so this sentence was sending a modeller to a *"Heron AI ribbon"* that no longer exists — and unlike [row 2](#), this message is **live**: it is what they read every time a write is refused. The path now reads *"the padlock button under Heron > AI Bridge"*. **STILL OPEN**: whether the sentence should name the button by its label (`Changes`) or keep describing the padlock is wording, and the owner's. | **6** | **TWO CLASSES DEFINE THE WORKFLOW ID, AND THE ONE THE DOCUMENTATION DESCRIBES IS THE ONE NOBODY CALLS.** [`HeronIdentity.NewWorkflowId()`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronIdentity.cs) returns `wf-20260827-192001-0003-a1b2` — time-prefixed, sortable, *"skimmable by eye"*, and its class docstring claims it owns identity under Golden Rule 10, *"the correlation key that ties one sentence to every agent, retrieval, model call and element touched (docs/21 section 13)"*. [`HeronAudit.NewWorkflowId()`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronAudit.cs) returns a bare 12-character GUID slice with no time in it. **A grep of every `.cs` in the repository finds `HeronIdentity.NewWorkflowId` called ZERO times**; the two live callers — [`RevitDispatcher.cs:187`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) and [`RevitWrite.cs:310`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) — both use `HeronAudit`'s. **SO THE AUDIT TRAIL IS NOT KEYED THE WAY IT IS DOCUMENTED.** Nothing is broken by it — the ids are unique enough (48 bits; a collision needs millions of requests) and correlation still works. What is lost is the property the format was designed for: `audit-YYYYMM.jsonl` cannot be sorted or skimmed by workflow id, which is the one thing a time prefix buys. **THIS IS THE EXACT HAZARD A SIBLING FILE WARNS ABOUT IN ITS OWN WORDS.** [`HeronPermissions.cs:150-154`](../platform/Heron.Core/HeronPermissions.cs) keeps `SetWriteEnabled` in one place so that a caller writing the key itself is not *"a second definition of this setting, and the day the two disagree the button reports a state the permission gate does not honour."* Identity has that second definition already. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the Python side mints workflow ids in a third format — the grep covered `.cs` only. **THE REPAIR IS A DELETION, NOT AN ADDITION**: pick one owner. If the time prefix is wanted, point the two callers at `HeronIdentity` and delete `HeronAudit`'s; if it is not, delete `HeronIdentity`'s and correct its class docstring, which currently describes a key nothing produces | **FIXED 2026-09-21 by deleting the one the documentation does not describe.** `HeronAudit.NewWorkflowId` is gone; both callers - `RevitDispatcher.cs` and `RevitWrite.cs` - now call `HeronIdentity.NewWorkflowId()`, and the audit trail is keyed the way `HeronIdentity`'s own docstring and Golden Rule 10 say it is. The deletion is the repair this row asked for, and the choice between the two was made by the truth hierarchy rather than by taste: the specification describes a sortable correlation key, and code never silently rewrites policy. **THE ANSWER TO THIS ROW'S OPEN QUESTION IS NO:** the Python side mints no workflow id at all - `heron_workflow.py` receives one and uses it as a filename component, which `wf-20260827-192001-0003-a1b2` is safe as. `tests/test_kernel.py` checks the shape, that five in a row differ, and the one property the time prefix is FOR - sorting them as text returns them in the order they were minted |

| **10** | **`FailureSeverity.DocumentCorruption` — REVIT'S HIGHEST SEVERITY — IS NEITHER ROLLED BACK NOR COUNTED, IN THE ONLY CODE THAT CAN CHANGE A MODEL.** [`RevitWrite.cs:899-926`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs), `CollectWarnings.PreprocessFailures`. The first loop returns `ProceedWithRollBack` for `FailureSeverity.Error`; the second counts and deletes `FailureSeverity.Warning`; anything that is neither falls through to **`FailureProcessingResult.Continue`**. **MEASURED AGAINST THE SHIPPED ASSEMBLIES, NOT FROM MEMORY** — the standard [`DocumentKey`'s own docstring](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) sets four hundred lines up. Reading `Autodesk.Revit.DB.FailureSeverity` out of `RevitAPI.dll` by reflection: **`None=0, Warning=1, Error=2, DocumentCorruption=3`** — identical in **Revit 2020 and Revit 2024**. So the case that falls through is the one *above* `Error`. **THE FILE'S OWN REASONING ARGUES AGAINST WHAT IT DOES.** *"Errors are different and are never swallowed: an error means Revit could not do what was asked, and continuing past one produces a model that is subtly wrong rather than obviously unchanged. Those roll the whole thing back."* Document corruption is where continuing is worst, and it is the one severity that continues — **and it is not counted either**, so the reply's `warnings: 0` and the audit's `warnings` field both say nothing happened. **NOTHING IN THE REPOSITORY MENTIONS IT.** A grep for `DocumentCorruption` across every `.cs`, `.py` and `.md` outside the handover archive returns **nothing**, and this is the only `IFailuresPreprocessor` Heron has. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** how readily Revit raises this severity during an element move — likely rare, and nothing here measures it; whether Revit would permit the commit afterwards regardless; and **Revit 2027**, whose `RevitAPI.dll` targets .NET 10 and could not be read by the .NET Framework reflection route used here — so 2027 is stated as **unchecked**, not as agreeing. **THE REPAIR IS ONE COMPARISON**: treat `>= FailureSeverity.Error` as roll-back, rather than `== Error` | **FIXED 2026-09-20, on the owner's instruction. One character: `==` became `>=`.** The comparison is `message.GetSeverity() >= FailureSeverity.Error`, so `DocumentCorruption` now rolls the whole group back, and a severity added **above** these in a future release is caught by arriving rather than by somebody remembering to add a branch for it. The docstring carries the enum values and the two releases they were read from, so the next reader does not have to go and look. **Compiled on all eight releases, 2020 through 2027, exit 0. NOT YET RUN IN REVIT**, and provoking a genuine `DocumentCorruption` to watch it roll back is not something this sweep can arrange |
| **11** | **THE "MODEL MOVED ON" REFUSAL REPORTS THE ONE NUMBER THE SAME FILE SAYS CANNOT TELL THE DIFFERENCE.** [`RevitWrite.cs:292-299`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs). The check is right: `now.SetEquals(preview.Ids)` compares the **id set**, exactly as the `Preview` class demands — *"a count alone cannot tell the difference between 'nothing changed' and 'one duct was deleted while another was drawn'. Both give 247. Only one of them is the model the user looked at."* **The message underneath it then reports counts**: *"it described "+ preview.Ids.Count +" "+ preview.Category +" and there are now "+ now.Count*. **In the exact case the docstring names — one deleted, one drawn — that renders as *"it described 247 ducts and there are now 247. Nothing was changed."*** A modeller reads a refusal that gives two identical numbers as its reason and concludes Heron is broken, at the moment it is in fact protecting them correctly. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the Python side rewrites this message before the user sees it. **THE REPAIR IS TO SAY WHAT THE SET CHECK ACTUALLY FOUND** — how many are new and how many are gone, both derivable from the two sets already in hand | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - the refusal now reports what the set check found.** It counts how many of the preview's ids are gone and how many are new, both from the two sets already in hand, and says so in words: *"It described 247 ducts, and one of them is gone, and one new one has appeared."* The deleted-one-drawn-one case that used to render as two identical numbers is the case it now names. Singular and plural are both written out. **NOT RUN IN REVIT** - it needs a model changing under a live preview, which is a Windows sitting |
| **12** | **THE AUDIT RECORDS WHICH ELEMENTS WERE *ATTEMPTED*, UNDER A COMMENT PROMISING WHICH ONES *MOVED*.** [`RevitWrite.cs:386-419`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) writes `elements` from `UniqueIds(doc, movable)` — the list assembled *before* the transaction. The comment above it says the ids exist to answer *"WHICH ducts did it move?"*. When `blocked` or `unverified` is non-zero, some of those ids did not move, and the record cannot say which. The same block is scrupulous about this distinction in the **counts** — `moved`, `attempted`, `partly`, `blocked`, `unverified` are five separate fields precisely because *"an audit that says 'moved 5' when 5 did not budge is worse than no audit"* — and then writes one undifferentiated id list. `Verify` already knows the answer per element and discards it, keeping only totals | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - `Verify` keeps the answer it already worked out.** A small `Verdicts` holder takes the id in each of its four branches, and the audit's `elements` field is now the ids that actually MOVED, which is what the comment above it has always promised. **The ordinary line is unchanged in size**: when everything attempted moved, nothing extra is written. When they differ, `attemptedElements` is added, plus `partlyElements`, `blockedElements` and `unverifiedElements` for whichever are non-empty - so the record says by name which ids did not move rather than leaving a reader to infer it from five counts. **NOT RUN IN REVIT**: a partial move needs a pinned or blocked element in a real model |
| **13** | **`DocumentKey` MIXES IDENTITY WITH LOCATION, SO A `SAVE AS` DURING A PREVIEW IS REPORTED AS THE MODEL HAVING BEEN CLOSED.** [`RevitWrite.cs:784-794`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs) returns `identity + "\|" + doc.PathName + "\|" + doc.Title`, where `identity` is the Project Information `UniqueId` — which its own docstring says *"survives being saved, renamed and moved"*. Appending the path and the title takes that property back out: after a `Save As` the key changes, the Golden Rule 20 comparison at line 251 fails, `IsStillOpen` compares the same composite key and returns false, and the user is told **"X has been closed since that preview"** about a model that is open in front of them under a new name. The file itself says why that wording was chosen with care — telling someone to click back to a model that is not open *"sends them looking for something that does not exist, at the exact moment they are already unsure what just happened to their work"*. Here it does the reverse and is equally wrong. **The title and path are already carried separately on the `Preview` object** for the wording, so nothing is lost by keying on identity alone. **Narrow** — it needs a `Save As` inside the two-minute preview window — and it **fails safe**: nothing is written | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - `DocumentKey` is the identity and nothing else.** The path and the title are gone from it, so a `Save As` inside the preview window no longer changes the key, and the Golden Rule 20 comparison and `IsStillOpen` both go on matching the model in front of the user. The title and path are carried on the `Preview` separately for the wording, so no refusal lost a word. **ONE BRANCH IS STILL LOCATION AND IT SAYS SO**: a document with no Project Information - a family, which Heron never writes to - still falls back to path and title, because the alternative is every such document sharing one key. **NOT RUN IN REVIT**: proving it takes a `Save As` inside the two-minute window |

| **15** | **A `select_by_category` WITH NO CATEGORY RETURNS A .NET EXCEPTION MESSAGE, WHILE THE SENTENCE WRITTEN FOR THAT EXACT CASE SITS TWENTY LINES BELOW IT.** [`RevitOperations.cs:438`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitOperations.cs) does `foreach (var part in category.Split(','))` with no null check. [`Json.ReadString`](../revit/Heron.Bridge/Json.cs) returns **`null`** for an absent key — line 150, read rather than assumed — so a request that omits `category` dereferences null. [`RevitDispatcher.cs:298-305`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) catches it (*"Never let one bad request throw out of here"* — correctly, so Revit is never at risk) and returns `Json.Error("operation_failed", ex.Message)`. **What the modeller is shown is therefore: *"Object reference not set to an instance of an object."*** **THE EMPTY-STRING CASE IS HANDLED BEAUTIFULLY, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THIS A DEFECT AND NOT A GAP.** `""` splits to one empty part, is skipped by the `name.Length == 0` guard, reaches line 458 and returns *"No category was given. Try 'ducts', or several at once like 'pipes, pipe fittings'."* The author wrote the refusal, placed it two lines past the crash, and never reached it. **THE SHARED HELPER GUARDS IT TOO** — `ResolveCategory` opens with `string.IsNullOrEmpty(category)` — so the check exists twice in this file and neither copy runs first. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether the MCP server always sends the key, which would make this unreachable in normal use; the add-in's own rule is that *"the wire is not a place to extend trust"* ([`RevitWrite.cs:723`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitWrite.cs)). **THE REPAIR IS TO TREAT NULL AS EMPTY** at line 438 | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - null is treated as empty, one character.** `(category ?? "").Split(',')` lets a request with no `category` reach the refusal written for it twenty lines below: *"No category was given. Try 'ducts', or several at once like 'pipes, pipe fittings'."* A modeller sees that instead of *"Object reference not set to an instance of an object."* **NOT RUN IN REVIT** - the path needs the add-in loaded, and the change is the one the empty-string case already proves works |

| **18** | **THE BANNER'S "FORGET THE MODEL THAT CLOSED" COMPARES STRING *REFERENCES*, NOT NAMES — SO IT ALMOST NEVER FORGETS.** [`RevitDispatcher.ForgetActiveModel:167-172`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) is `Interlocked.CompareExchange(ref _activeModel, null, title)`. For a **reference type** that comparand is matched by **reference identity**, never by value — but the method's own docstring says the opposite: *"Cleared only when the model closing IS the one being named… **comparing the names** is the only way to tell those two apart from here."* **MEASURED, NOT REASONED.** A net8.0 program ran `ForgetActiveModel` exactly as written: with the **same instance** stored and passed back it clears (`cleared? True`); with **two equal-but-distinct** strings — `a == b` **True**, `ReferenceEquals(a, b)` **False** — it does **not** (`cleared? False`). **THE TWO STRINGS ARE ALWAYS DISTINCT IN PRACTICE, AND ONE OF THEM PROVABLY SO.** `_activeModel` is written from three places: [`HeronApplication.cs:421`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs) passes `document.Title` on view-activated, [`HeronApplication.cs:445`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/HeronApplication.cs) passes a **separate** `document.Title` read on document-closing, and [`RevitDispatcher.cs:318-320`](../revit/Heron.Revit.Addin/RevitDispatcher.cs) writes `Json.ReadString(response, "document")` — **a string parsed out of JSON, which is a fresh allocation every single time and can never be reference-equal to anything Revit hands back.** So after any job has run, the clear cannot possibly fire. **THE SAME API IS USED CORRECTLY FORTY LINES BELOW**, which is what makes this a slip rather than a misunderstanding: `RevitJob.TakeBannerEnd` at line 562 is `Interlocked.CompareExchange(ref _bannerEnded, 1, 0)` on an **`int`**, where the comparison is by value and the code is right. **WHAT IT COSTS IS THE FAILURE THE DOCSTRING DESCRIBES**: *"Closing the LAST model activates no other view, so nothing else would ever clear the name — and the banner would go on announcing a document nobody has open, while the operation underneath answered that there is no model at all. Two halves of one card contradicting each other is worse than a card naming nothing."* **Bounded**: the next job overwrites the name from its own answer, so the stale name survives only until the next request — but the case this guard exists for is precisely the one where no next request comes. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether Revit's `Document.Title` happens to return the same instance on two reads, which is the only thing that would rescue the `Title`-to-`Title` path; it needs Revit and it does not rescue the JSON path at all. Cosmetic — it misnames a card, it changes no model. **THE REPAIR IS TO COMPARE BY VALUE**: read, `==`, write, under the lock this class already has | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - compared by value, under a lock.** `_activeModel` now has its own `_modelLock` and every one of the four sites goes through it; `ForgetActiveModel` reads, compares with `string.Equals(..., Ordinal)` and clears. **A SECOND `Interlocked` FORM WOULD NOT HAVE DONE** - `CompareExchange` on a reference type matches by reference whatever is written beside it, which is how this passed review in the first place - so the repair is the lock the row asked for. **NOT RUN IN REVIT**: what would settle it is closing the last model after a job has run and watching the banner stop naming it |

| **27** | **THE RISK AND THE PROVIDER PRINTED BESIDE IT CAME FROM TWO DIFFERENT FRAGMENTS.** Found 2026-09-21 reading `mcp/server/heron_runtime.py` in the sweep. `_judge` computed `risk = _worst_risk(rows)` - correctly the HIGHEST across every provider, with its own docstring giving the reason - and then set `provider = rows[0].get("id")`, whichever happened to sort first. **SO A CAPABILITY ANSWERED BY A READ FRAGMENT AND A MODIFY ONE REPORTED `risk: MODIFY` BESIDE THE READ FRAGMENT'S NAME.** In the `BLOCKED_BY_TRUST` verdict that is the worst version of it: the sentence says *"MODIFY, and the ceiling here is EXECUTE"* and names a fragment that is not the one being blocked, so a planner - or a person reading the snapshot - would go and look at the wrong fragment. The same pair is returned for `NEEDS_REVIT` and `AVAILABLE`. **IT IS LATENT, AND THAT WAS CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED**: `tools/check-reachable.py` records `snapshot` as deliberately unwired - *"the provider for planning code that does not exist ... PROPOSALS.md F1"* - so nothing in production reads either field today. It is [row 1](#)'s shape exactly: a public function whose whole job is to be the answer, and the next caller will not know. **AND THE SUITE HAD THE CASE AND HALF THE CHECK.** `tests/test_runtime_snapshot.py` already builds the mixed READ/MODIFY capability under the heading *"The risk of a capability is its worst provider, never its first"* and asserted only the verdict. The missing half was the name. **THE REPAIR MAKES IT UNGETTABLE-WRONG RATHER THAN CORRECT ONCE:** `_worst_risk` becomes `_worst_provider` and returns the ROW, so the risk and the id are read off the same object and a caller cannot pair them up wrongly again; `_worst_risk` stays as a one-line wrapper for the two verdicts that want the figure alone. **MEASURED:** the new check fails against the previous implementation - reverting the file leaves the verdict green and the provider check red - and passes after. **WHAT WAS NOT CHECKED:** whether `brain/heron_capability.py`, which this file says it copies the rule from, pairs them correctly; it was not read in this sitting. | **FIXED 2026-09-21, in the sitting that found it.** Nothing is broken today, and the reason to fix it now rather than record it is that the repair is four lines and the alternative is a correct-looking pair of fields waiting for its first reader |

| **42** | **THE TOOL THAT ASKS WHETHER THE HOMEWORK WAS DONE COUNTED A GATE RECORDED AS `NOT RUN` AS DONE.** Found 2026-09-21 by watching it happen to this branch's own evidence. `tools/change-evidence.py` keeps **three** states apart on purpose - `PASS`, `FAIL`, `NOT RUN` with `exit: null` - and `tests/test_change_gate.py` already pinned the bottom half of that: *"no suites at all is NOT RUN, not PASS"*. Then `tools/check-change.py` asked `g not in ran` - **the presence of the KEY, never its VALUE** - so a gate the record carried as NOT RUN satisfied the obligation. **MEASURED, NOT ARGUED**: a capture whose `--tests` argument matched no suite recorded `tests: NOT RUN`, and this tool printed **PASS - "Scope matches the declared intent and every gate it owes was run"**. The line a reader sees was wrong in the same way: `Gates actually run:` listed every key in the record, `tests` among them. **THIS IS THE ONE TOOL THAT CANNOT HAVE THIS DEFECT** - every other gate answers a question about the code, and this one answers *did anybody ask the others*. It is the repository's own standing lesson at the top of the stack: a guard that fails open and says nothing is indistinguishable from a guard that passed | **FIXED 2026-09-21.** `unmet` now reads the RESULT: a gate counts as run only when it is `PASS` or `FAIL`, so NOT RUN is owed and unmet and the verdict is **REVISE**, naming it. The report separates the three: `Gates actually run` lists only what ran, a new `In the record, NOT RUN` line names what the record carries as unrun, and `Owed and not run` names what that costs. **A GATE THAT RAN AND FAILED IS STILL REVISE** - checked, because the obvious form of this fix quietly turns a failure into a missing gate and the two want different answers. **PINNED IN `tests/test_change_gate.py`**, which is where the bottom half of the rule already lived: four checks built on a written evidence record - every gate PASS, then `tests` NOT RUN, then `tests` FAIL. **Proved by making it fail**: with the previous `check-change.py` stashed back in, **3 of the new checks fail**; with the fix, all pass. **AND THE FIRST THING IT CAUGHT WAS THIS BRANCH** - the evidence record behind rows 37 to 41 said `tests: NOT RUN`, the gate had called it PASS, and after the fix it correctly demanded the suites be run. **THE TEST ITSELF WAS THEN WRONG IN A WAY WORTH RECORDING.** Its first version typed the five gates a medium-risk change owes into the synthetic record - and *owed* is not a constant: a diff touching `revit/` raises the `revit-version` signal, which owes `check-compile` and `check-api-surface` as well. So the suite went red on the very next commit to edit an add-in file, reporting *owed and not run* about gates the test had simply never listed. **A test whose verdict depends on the diff it happens to be run beside is not testing the thing it names.** The record is now built from `ALWAYS`, `BY_RISK` and `BY_SIGNAL` read out of the tool, so it holds every gate that could ever be owed whatever the tree looks like |

This is exit 3, which is NOT a pass: nothing was checked"* arrived on the failure list beside a suite that ran and broke - and the comment four lines above that loop names exactly the confusion it was causing: *"a lump pass total is how a real regression hides, so the build compares the SET of failures instead of counting them."* **THE SET CANNOT TELL "DID NOT RUN" FROM "RAN AND FAILED" EITHER.** Three suites are on the known-failure list for needing the MCP SDK or a built .NET host, and all three exit 3; **once a suite is excused, a genuine failure in it is invisible.** MEASURED BOTH WAYS on a three-suite fixture: flip the excused suite from exit 3 to exit 1 and today's logic reports nothing about it, while the three-way split puts it on the failure list AND names it as one that ran after all. **AND THE AGENT'S OWN DOCSTRING CLAIMED THE OPPOSITE** - *"the workflow's loop and this agent run the same suites the same way, and neither is generated from the other, which is a duplication worth naming rather than hiding"* - honest about the duplication and wrong about the one dimension the file exists for | **FIXED 2026-09-21 BY DELETING THE SECOND COPY RATHER THAN CORRECTING IT.** The job now runs `brain/heron_unit_test.py --json`, which is the sweep, and splits four ways: **failed** (red, whatever list it is on), **could not run** (compared against a list that is now a known-NOT-RUNNABLE list, not a known-failure one), **hung** (its own state - not a pass, not a failure, it never said anything), and passed. `--json` is new: the printed report is for a person and drops the two things a caller needs most, which is why the bash existed at all. **NOTHING RE-RUNS ANYTHING NOW.** The old job re-ran each new failure to recover the reason it had sent to `/dev/null` (rows 160 and 166); the sweep already keeps every failure's last line, so the reason arrives with the verdict. Gated in `tests/test_unit_test.py`: `--json` is valid JSON, carries the three lists, and a WAITING suite never appears among the failures - proved by making a fixture suite exit 3 and watching it stay out of `failed`. The docstring says what is now true: **one sweep, one place** |

| **69** | **A COMMENT INSIDE A `PROVEN` FRAGMENT'S IMPLEMENTATION IS NOT A FREE EDIT, AND I LEARNED IT BY HAVING CI REJECT MY OWN FIX.** Found 2026-09-21 the expensive way. One waits today |

| **72** | **THE FUNCTION WHOSE DOCSTRING SAYS IT ANSWERS *ALIVE*, *DEAD* OR *CANNOT TELL* HAD A FOURTH OUTCOME: NEVER.** Found 2026-09-21 sweeping `brain/` and `mcp/` for unbounded calls. `mcp/client/heron_bridge_client.py`'s `process_is_running()` shells out to `tasklist` with `subprocess.check_output` and **no timeout**, under a docstring that reads ***"Returns None when it cannot tell - and 'cannot tell' must never be treated as 'dead'."*** A hang is not None. It is never. **AND IT IS THREE CALLS, NOT ONE**: `bridge_process_is_running()` asks once per image in `BRIDGE_IMAGES` - `Revit.exe`, the test host, `dotnet.exe` - so a stalled `tasklist` is three unbounded waits per process id, and the session picker asks per discovered bridge. **WHAT MAKES IT WORTH A ROW IS THE COMPANY IT KEEPS.** Every pipe operation in that same file is bounded on purpose: `_connect` runs a deadline loop and returns None when it expires, `_read_line` runs the read on a **daemon thread** and joins with a timeout, `RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_S` comes from `heron_config.response_timeout()` and four call sites pass `fragment_timeout()`. The one subprocess call in the file is the one that was not - and it is on the path a modeller waits on. `tools/check-gaps.py` already carries the lesson one layer out, `SUITE_TIMEOUT = 300`, with its own comment: *"a test that hangs is work somebody can do today"* | **FIXED 2026-09-21 WITH ONE KEYWORD, BECAUSE THE EXISTING HANDLER ALREADY DID THE RIGHT THING WITH IT.** `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` is an `Exception`, and the call was already wrapped in `except Exception: return None` - so `timeout=TASKLIST_TIMEOUT_S` turns a hang into exactly the answer the docstring promises. **Five seconds and not one**: `tasklist` on a loaded machine is slow rather than instant, and a deadline that fired on a healthy box would call a live Revit unknown and prune nothing. **Not a config key**, deliberately: eight settings are declared in `HeronConfig.Defaults` and read back by a second reader, and [row 5b-29](#) is what a ninth costs when the two drift. **MEASURED FIRST, AND THE FIRST COUNT WAS WRONG**: a line-scoped grep said 55 of 56 `subprocess` calls carry no timeout; an AST scan says **40 of 56** - the grep missed every call whose `timeout=` sits on the next line, `change-evidence.py` among them. The other 39 are developer tools and suites, where a hang is visible at a terminal and CI has a job timeout. Reported as **one place**, not as a sweep |
| **73** | **THE TOOL THAT CAPTURES EVIDENCE CANNOT RUN TWO OF THE GATES THE TOOL THAT READS IT DEMANDS, SO EVERY C# CHANGE COMES BACK `REVISE`.** Found 2026-09-21 running the gate on PR #228. `check-change.py`'s `BY_SIGNAL` owes `["check-compile", "check-api-surface"]` for a **revit-version** signal - which any change touching a `.cs` file or `Directory.Build.props` raises - and `change-evidence.py`'s `RUNNABLE` table held eight gates, neither of them among them. **MEASURED**: `REVISE - gate(s) this change owes were not run: check-package, check-compile, check-api-surface`. **THE ESCAPE HATCH IS THE WRONG ONE.** `--stated` exists for a gate that ran **somewhere else**, and marks it *as a claim rather than a measurement* - so the only route through was to file a real local measurement as a claim, which is the one use that flag exists not to have. **AND BOTH RUN HERE**: `check-api-surface` answers in seconds with its assemblies cached in `tools/api-surface/.assemblies` - 362 referenced Revit types and members, exit 0 - and `check-compile` builds all eight releases in about four minutes. **TWO TOOLS DESIGNED AS A PAIR, AND ONE COULD NOT PRODUCE WHAT THE OTHER REQUIRED** | **FIXED 2026-09-21 - two lines in `RUNNABLE`, and nothing gets slower by default.** `--gate` is opt-in, so the change is not that these now run, it is that it becomes **possible** to record what the other tool requires. | **78** | **THE INSTALLER READS POWERSHELL BACK WITH A CALL THAT DEADLOCKS, AND THE TEN-MINUTE CEILING ABOVE IT CANNOT FIRE BECAUSE THE THREAD NEVER REACHES IT.** Found 2026-09-21 reading `platform/Heron.Installer/WindowsAdapters.cs`, new in #233 and never run. `PowerShellRunner.Run` redirects both streams and then takes them one after the other - `text.Append(process.StandardOutput.ReadToEnd()); text.Append(process.StandardError.ReadToEnd());` - and only afterwards calls `WaitForExit(timeoutSeconds * 1000)`. **A pipe holds a few kilobytes.** A child that fills the one nobody is reading blocks there, so it never closes the other, so the first `ReadToEnd` never returns - and the ceiling on the next line is not late, it is **unreachable**, because the thread is stuck above it. **MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSERTED**, same pattern, same runtime, on this machine: **8 KB** on standard error came back in **0.0s**; **200 KB** never came back at all and a **10-second** ceiling never fired; 2,000 KB the same. A watchdog had to kill it. **THE CALLER THAT REACHES IT IS THE DEPLOY.** `DeployScriptDeployer` allows **600 seconds** for `tools/deploy-addin.ps1` - 578 lines, `$ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"` - so a file Revit still has open puts a PowerShell error record on standard error while `Write-Host` is filling standard output. That is an **installer window hung with no way out**, on the one run where something already went wrong. **AND THE SAME METHOD CARRIES A SECOND FAULT FOR THE SAME REASON**: both streams go into **one** `StringBuilder`, and `PowerShellRevitEnvironment.Look()` then calls `JsonDocument.Parse(run.Output)` on the join. **One line on standard error lands in the middle of the JSON**, the parse throws, `Look()` returns null - and from there `InstalledReleases()` is empty, so the window says *“No Revit was found on this PC”* to somebody who has three, while `RunningRevits()` returns the unknown-release sentinel that **blocks every install**. Two consumers wanted different things from one buffer | **FIXED 2026-09-21, AND THE FIX IS THE ONE THE RUNTIME DOCUMENTS RATHER THAN A BIGGER TIMEOUT.** Both streams are drained at once - `OutputDataReceived` and `ErrorDataReceived` with `BeginOutputReadLine` / `BeginErrorReadLine` - into **two** builders, so neither can fill while the other is being read. `Result` now carries `StandardOutput` beside `Output`: `Look()` parses standard output **alone**, and `LastMeaningfulLine` still reads both, which is what a person would have seen. **THE SECOND `WaitForExit()` IS NOT BELT AND BRACES**: the overload that takes a timeout returns as soon as the process is gone, which can be before the reader has handed over the last of what it read, so the parameterless one follows it - without that the tail goes missing at random, and JSON with its tail missing does not parse. **SAME MEASUREMENT AFTER**: 200 KB returns in **0.0s** and 2,000 KB in **0.1s**, with stdout and stderr separated - 17 bytes against 2,048,001. `tests/test_installer_adapters.py` holds the rule and **was shown to fail**: run against the file as #233 shipped it, **seven of its eight checks go red**. It cannot run the code - `powershell.exe`, and this is Linux - and says so; whether it talks to a real PowerShell is still owed on Windows, AA7 to AA9 |
| **79** | **HERON'S ONLY WAY BACK FROM A BAD INSTALL DELETES ITSELF BEFORE ITS REPLACEMENT EXISTS, AND THE ROLLBACK THEN RESTORES WHATEVER IS LEFT WITHOUT CHECKING IT IS WHOLE.** Found 2026-09-21 reading `tools/deploy-addin.ps1` after #233 rewrote it. `Save-PreviousInstall` ran `Remove-Item $backupDir -Recurse -Force` **first**, then `New-Item`, then a recursive `Copy-Item` of the live install into the empty folder. **The one artifact that can undo a bad update is spent before the second one exists**, and a copy that dies halfway - a full disk, a file something still has open, a lid closed - leaves **part** of an install where a whole one was, with nothing anywhere saying so. **THE ROLLBACK THEN TRUSTS IT.** `-Rollback` checked only that the folder was there; `replaced.json` was read `if (Test-Path ...)` purely to **print** the date and file count, and the restore went ahead either way. So a half-written backup is put back over the live install, and the verify afterwards passes, because **the main assembly is the first thing copied** and that is all it looks for. Revit then refuses the add-in and names nothing - the exact symptom four other guards in this same script exist to keep away from a modeller, each added after it happened once. **THIS IS [ROW 5b-31](#)'s SHAPE**, the checkpoint save that deleted the old file before renaming the new one, **in the script whose own header says rollback must be TESTED rather than implemented** and cites `brain/heron_update.py` refusing a release with no recorded `rollback_tested`. **AND `replaced.json` WAS ALREADY THE ANSWER.** It is written **last**, after the copy, so its presence already meant *this backup finished* - nothing was reading it that way | **FIXED 2026-09-21, BOTH HALVES, AND THE SECOND HALF COST NOTHING TO BUILD BECAUSE THE MARK WAS ALREADY THERE.** `Save-PreviousInstall` now copies into `$backupDir.incomplete`, writes `replaced.json` into it, and only **then** removes the old backup and renames the staged one into place. **It is not called atomic**: the swap is a rename inside one folder, which is quick, and the recursive copy that used to sit in that window is not - that is the whole of the claim, said in the comment, because row 5b-31 was a comment claiming an atomic swap over a delete-then-rename. `-Rollback` refuses a backup with **no `replaced.json`** and one whose **file count no longer matches its own record**, both **before** the live install is touched, each naming what to do instead. **AND MY OWN FIRST NEGATIVE TEST PASSED WHEN IT SHOULD HAVE FAILED**, which is half of why this row is worth reading. The new ordering check was `text.find(a) < text.find(b)` - and `str.find` returns **-1** for a string that is not there, and **-1 is less than every real position**, so the check went **green loudest exactly when the guard it was checking had been deleted**. Caught by breaking the guard on purpose and watching nothing happen. Rewritten to assert both positions exist first. **Four negative tests now, one rule each** - completion check removed, swap turned back into a copy, staging folder pointed at the backup, count refusal reworded - and **all four go red**. Still not run: this is a text check on PowerShell from Linux, the rollback proof of 2026-09-19 stays **STALE** for this file, and the real thing is owed on Windows |

| **83** | **NINETY OF THE 145 MODULES IN `brain/` ARE IMPORTED BY NOTHING BUT THEIR OWN TEST - AND THAT IS THE EXACT SHAPE `mcp/README.md` SAYS WAS FIXED ON 2026-08-29, WHEN IT WAS EIGHT.** Measured 2026-09-21 with an AST walk over every `.py` in `brain`, `mcp`, `tools`, `tests` and `.claude`, resolving each import to the module it names. **145 modules in `brain/`: 53 reachable from `mcp/` or from another brain module, 2 reached only by a tool, 90 imported only by their own test, and 0 imported by nothing at all.** **THE COMPARISON IS THE REASON THIS IS A ROW.** `mcp/README.md` describes the state before `heron_brain.py` existed in its own words: the transport-only rule *“was being kept by **having no route at all**: eight brain modules, seven fragments and ten skills, imported by nothing but their own tests, and therefore **unreachable from any conversation**”*. One named seam was built so the rule could hold without that. The seam works - [row 5b-82](#) measured ten MCP tools standing on it. **The number it was built to fix has gone from eight to ninety.** **AND NOTHING DISPATCHES DYNAMICALLY, SO THE COUNT IS NOT AN ARTEFACT OF THE METHOD.** Three `importlib` uses exist in non-test code and all three load one named file - `heron_agent_count`, a backup tool, a spec probe. There is no registry that resolves an arbitrary brain module by name, so an import really is the only route in. **WHAT THE NINETY LOOK LIKE.** Every one carries a `Heron-Agent:` header, so each is the Kernel half of an agent in [28](28-agent-registry.md) - and some of them are operationally serious rather than incidental: `heron_safemode`, `heron_rollback`, `heron_healing`, `heron_deployment` (which [row 5b-75](#) found promoting fragments through a lifecycle nothing calls), `heron_shadow`, `heron_regression`, `heron_observability`. **WHAT IS DELIBERATELY NOT CLAIMED HERE: THAT ANY OF IT IS WRONG.** An agent may be a vocabulary for who owns what rather than a runtime actor, and a library waiting for its caller is not the same thing as dead code. Answering that needs [18 - the Agent Operating System](18-agent-operating-system.md) and [28](28-agent-registry.md) read properly, and **neither has been read by this sweep**. [Row 5b-75](#) is what it costs to settle a question like this on half the sources - four modules were changed to match a document whose own header said it was a proposal, and all four had to be reverted | **OPEN — needs a sitting. NOTHING WAS CHANGED AND NO MODULE WAS WIRED TO ANYTHING.** **The measurement is the deliverable**; the judgement is not mine to make from here. **So it ships as a command rather than as a number in a cell** - `python tools/module-reach.py`, `--list` to name every module in each bucket. It walks every `.py` in `brain`, `mcp`, `tools`, `tests` and `.claude`, resolves each `import` and `from ... import` to a `brain/` module, and buckets by who the importer is. **It is a REPORT and exits 0 whatever it finds**, because a finding here is a question for a person and not a broken build - and it says so in its own closing lines rather than leaving the next reader to wonder why it went green. **Three questions for the sitting, in the order that decides the rest.** (1) **Is a brain module meant to be reachable at runtime at all**, or is `brain/` a library the fragment and capability layers draw on, with the agent header naming an owner rather than a caller? (2) If they are meant to be reachable, **what is the route** - a runner, the way [row 141](#) says skills have none, or more seams like `heron_brain.py`? (3) **Which of the ninety are load-bearing today**, because `heron_safemode` and `heron_rollback` being uncalled means something different from `heron_onboarding` being uncalled. **This is the first OPEN row in section 5b**, and it is open on purpose: every other row here was a thing that could be settled by reading or measuring, and this one cannot be **The measurement it turns on got sharper on 2026-09-22 and the QUESTION DID NOT MOVE** - see row 5b-126. `module-reach.py` counted one hop, so a module imported by a neighbour read as reached whether or not the neighbour was; following the imports gives **17 of 145 reachable from `mcp/`**, and **128 that no conversation can arrive at** rather than the ninety below. The three questions are unchanged and all three are still yours |

nothing ever flagged, **3 red**; the clause trimmed to 60 characters, **1 red**. **CHECKED AND DISCARDED, and it is the near miss worth recording**: `screen()` caps each REPORTED finding at 80 characters with an ellipsis, which looks like an R-82 violation and is not - it scans the whole text unwindowed, the clause is carried in full, and the cap is on the visible excerpt of what matched. **A rule about the scanner's window is not a rule about the report's margin**, and writing that row would have been a finding manufactured out of a word |
